Motivated by this failure mode, we propose score-guided aggregate-validated mixed precision (SGAV-MP), a post-training compression framework that selects layer precisions under reliability constraints. SGAV-MP ranks quantization actions with a compact benefit-risk score and accepts them only after full-model reliability validation on a calibration set. Across five 2D backbones and three 3D benchmarks, SGAV-MP preserves FP32-level Dice and high uncertainty-map agreement, achieving $3.59\times$--$7.8\times$ compression in 2D and $4.73\times$--$7.82\times$ in 3D. We also report backend-supported TensorRT deployment measurements separately to distinguish runtime execution from reliability-preserving storage compression.

摘要：

##### **ViCoR: Estimating Visual Necessity via Counterfactual Residuals for Multimodal Medical Data Selection**
2610.25202v1 by Haotian Gan, Yudong Li, Xintian Li, Jiarui Han, Weidong Tang

Medical vision-language models often exhibit hidden stratification, where high overall performance masks failures on subtle visual findings and overlapping clinical conditions. We trace this failure mode to modality imbalance during fine-tuning, where strong linguistic priors can suppress visually grounded learning and induce plausible but weakly grounded rationales. Existing data selection methods usually score image-text pairs as holistic units, without separating visually necessary samples from shortcut-solvable ones. To address this problem, we propose ViCoR, a counterfactual-residual-based data selection method for Med-VLM fine-tuning. ViCoR estimates visual necessity through image-dependent optimization residuals, calibrates the resulting scores with medical supervision compatibility, and constructs a diversity-aware fine-tuning subset. Across two backbones, four external generalization benchmarks, and one multi-disease robustness benchmark, ViCoR-selected 20\% subsets achieve the strongest average performance compared with full-data fine-tuning and selection baselines. Gradient-level analysis and perturbation audits further show that ViCoR enriches image-dependent training signals and strengthens image-level and evidence-region dependence. The code will be available at https://github.com/htgan-ai/ViCoR.

摘要：

##### **ClinSDT: LLM-Encoded Clinical Semantic Guidance for Sepsis Treatment via Decision Transformer**
2610.25121v1 by Yanyan Wu, Guanyu Zhu, Changbin Zhao, Jin Zhang, Dexu Yu, Tianmin Sun, Jining Luan, Chunxiao Li, Hanwen Du, Youhua Li

Offline sepsis treatment aims to learn treatment policies from retrospective ICU trajectories. Decision Transformer-based methods provide a natural sequence modeling framework for this task, but they mainly rely on raw physiological states and historical clinician actions. Such raw trajectory tokens may not explicitly capture clinical semantics that clinicians use in practice, such as patient severity, recent treatment response, and current risk priorities. To address this limitation, we propose ClinSDT, an LLM-assisted Decision Transformer framework for offline sepsis treatment. ClinSDT uses a frozen LLM as a semantic bridge rather than a treatment decision maker. It constructs three clinical semantic views from ICU trajectories, namely Profile, Context, and Advisory, and inserts their encoded representations as independent tokens into a Decision Transformer policy. To support policy refinement and rollout, ClinSDT further trains a Treatment-Response Forecaster to estimate the next physiological state, SAPS-II change, and predictive uncertainty from state-action inputs. In the second training stage, the forecaster screens local counterfactual treatment candidates, and only low-uncertainty candidates with lower predicted SAPS-II increase are distilled into the policy after semantic regeneration. During inference, ClinSDT performs closed-loop treatment rollout by refreshing semantic inputs from the currently available patient state. Experiments on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV show that ClinSDT achieves consistent relative improvements over representative imitation learning, offline reinforcement learning, and Decision Transformer baselines under the same rollout protocol. Code and data are available at https://github.com/KAG778/ClinSDT.

|**2026-10-16**|**AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery**|Shicheng Liu et.al.|[2610.23845v1](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23845v1)|null|

#### Abstracts
##### **Center-of-Mass Interpretation of Evolution Strategies**
2610.35865v1 by Akshat Gupta

Evolution strategies (ES) update a model by sampling a population of perturbed copies, scoring each one, and moving in the reward-weighted direction of the perturbations. This update is usually presented as an estimate of a smoothed gradient. We present a geometrical interpretation of this update instead. Treat each perturbed model as a particle and its reward as the particle's mass. Before scoring, every particle has the same mass and the population balances at its centroid. Once rewards are assigned, it balances at its reward-weighted center of mass instead. We show that the mean-subtracted ES update is exactly this displacement, scaled by a scalar factor that depends on the mean reward, for any finite population. The physical reading needs nonnegative rewards, which can always be arranged by adding a constant to every reward without changing the update. In the limit of a large population or under antithetic sampling, the centroid sits on the model itself, so ES has an even simpler geometric interpretation: from its current location, the model moves towards the reward-weighted center of mass of its own population. The same picture explains what subtracting the mean reward does: it changes the point from which the displacement is measured, from the model to the sampled centroid. Our contribution is an intuitive, geometric way to visualize an ES update, exact for any finite population.

##### **CLOSURE: Cross-Channel Closure for Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detection**
2610.35753v1 by Fan Xu, Hao Wu, Zhi Song, Hanbo Huang, Wenzhuo Ma, Nan Wang, Wei Gong, Xibin Zhao

Multivariate time series produced by industrial installations and server clusters are under continuous monitoring, and anomaly detection is the first line of defense before a local fault escalates into a system-wide failure. In the absence of labels, the dominant paradigm trains a reconstruction model on normal data and scores the residual to reach a verdict. Recent work has steadily enriched the expressiveness of the recovery map through Transformers, diffusion models, and cross-scale associations. A residual, however, only certifies that an observation can be recovered; it does not certify that the variables still explain one another as they do under normal operation. A coordinated yet unfamiliar operating transition inflates the residual without breaking any relationship, whereas a sensor drifting away from its peers may leave the residual silent. Methods that fold inter-variable dependencies into the reconstruction objective inherit this ambiguity, because their relational evidence is ultimately supervised by the same recovery loss. We therefore propose CLOSURE, which evaluates recoverability and cross-channel consistency as two normal-data prediction tasks learned in separation. The recoverability task is solved by a diffusion reconstructor conditioned on a discrete memory of normal temporal bases. The consistency task is solved by a relation-closure operator that predicts every channel exclusively from the others, where the target's own trajectory is withheld and sparse signed gates decide which peers are actually used. The two scores are reconciled only through calibration on normal data. Extensive experiments show that CLOSURE consistently outperforms recent reconstruction-, frequency-, and topology-based detectors on public benchmark streams, confirming the value of examining consistency alongside recoverability.

Augmented large language model (LLM) systems have reported high benchmark accuracies in rare disease diagnosis, yet their real-world clinical utility remains unproven. A recent meta-analysis flagged augmented LLM diagnostic evaluations as having a high risk of bias due to suspected data leakage; however, the precise impact of these vulnerabilities on model inference has yet to be fully quantified. In this study, we present a comprehensive empirical audit to quantify evaluation artifacts across prevailing AI paradigms, using a repository of over 60,000 cases from diverse public and independent clinical cohorts. By reconstructing a state-of-the-art agentic framework, we demonstrate that candidate-space contamination artificially inflates performance: enforcing strict cross-dataset isolation resulted in a marked performance decline, with the cross-dataset mean Recall@1 decreasing from 54.3% (under conventional one-vs-rest evaluation) to 40.3% (Hedges' g = 0.94). Similar spatial overfitting in supervised classifiers caused diagnostic accuracy to drop from an internal mean of 42.9% to 16.7% across external cohorts (Cohen's d = 2.01). Furthermore, we quantify how LLMs infer diagnoses by exploiting retrospective therapeutic cues in clinical narratives, and how temporal misalignment in foundational knowledge bases leads to performance fluctuations. We conclude that prevailing evaluation protocols for augmented LLM systems inadvertently reflect data leakage rather than genuine medical reasoning. To address these systematic artifacts, we propose a rigorous evaluation framework - coupling strict data-isolation protocols with standardized benchmarking safeguards - to ensure future diagnostic AI for rare diseases is evaluated on actual clinical robustness.

Lung tumor segmentation plays a vital role in early detection and treatment planning of lung cancer. Although state-of-the-art deep learning models achieve high dice score coefficients (DSC), each forward pass requires computing large amounts of floating point operations, making them computationally expensive, requiring GPU acceleration, and having slow inference speed, limiting real-world clinical deployment. We propose a lightweight, energy-efficient model that leverages the ENet architecture for its real-time inference capability and low computational cost, enhanced with UNet-style skip connections between encoder and decoder stages for feature reuse and the lightweight Convolutional Block Attention Module (CBAM) for improved feature representation. The model is trained on publicly available data from The Cancer Imaging Archive (TCIA) via the Medical Segmentation Decathlon Challenge (MSD) and evaluated on the Lung Image Database Consortium and Image Database Resource Initiative (LIDC-IDRI) dataset. Our model achieves a DSC of 0.90812 (threshold of 0.5) with its compact architecture of 430,052 parameters and 0.549 Giga Multiply-Accumulate operations (GMAC), making it up to 10 times faster, requires 79 times less GMAC than UNet, and has 22 times fewer parameters than SegNet. These characteristics make it well-suited for deployment in clinical settings where computational resources are limited and decrease the cost of utilizing such a system. Additionally, the explainable AI technique of Grad-CAM is used to provide visual interpretability of the model's predictions and to identify regions where segmentation performance requires improvement.

##### **Data Optimization for LLMs: A Survey**
2610.33405v1 by Ou Wu

Large language models (LLMs) have attracted great attention in both academic and industrial communities in recent years. Numerous survey studies have covered various aspects of LLMs, such as learning strategies, applications, alignment, explainability, and evaluation. However, little headway has been made in reviewing data-related aspects for LLMs, despite data being recognized as a key factor contributing to the emergence of the LLMs era. A wide range of data optimization algorithms and techniques are employed in LLMs' training and application processes. These techniques often originate from diverse deep learning domains, with their theoretical inspirations and heuristic motivations appearing unrelated to each other. This study aims to develop a comprehensive framework for LLMs' data optimization techniques to enhance data utilization and efficiency. We first discuss data challenges in LLMs' training and inference. Second, we summarize data perception dimensions for evaluating LLMs' data properties. These dimensions primarily include quality, difficulty, diversity, and redundancy, laying a foundation for subsequent optimization. Third, existing optimization techniques are reviewed and categorized into four groups: data selection, augmentation, reweighting, and others. Fourth, we analyze interconnections between these categories. Finally, we identify key challenges and future directions for LLMs' data optimization compared with conventional deep learning approaches.

##### **TempShap: When and How Much a Feature Matters in Time-Series Forecasting**
2610.33330v1 by Chi Zhao, Anastasiia Chelnokova, Jing Liu, Elena Parilina

Attribution for a time-series forecaster should answer two questions that a feature-level scalar cannot: when in the input window a feature acts, and how much its influence changes across the forecast horizon. Most existing Shapley explainers treat each feature as one player and therefore collapse both axes. We propose TempShap, a retrospective, loss-based Shapley framework whose players are the individual input cells $(t,i)$. Its attribution tensor yields temporal-localization and horizon-impact profiles. We validate these against planted temporal structures, a tractable exact cell game, and a closed-form attribution oracle for a real linear forecaster. Permutation sharing estimates the cell-level game at localized perturbation cost. Under redundant inputs, one-at-a-time scores under-credit substitutable information, whereas the signed Shapley decomposition preserves total value and yields more reliable held-out deletion rankings under mean masking. A planted counterexample isolates the redundancy mechanism. Experiments show a deletion-ranking advantage across Shapley estimators, forecasting benchmarks, and model families. TempShap therefore makes fine-grained Shapley attribution both practical and operationally useful.

The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. 2610.33112v1 by John deVadoss

Use-based AI regulation assigns obligations by deployment risk but lacks an evidentiary object to carry them. ##### **A Mathematical Theory of the Jacobian Lens**

Recent work from Anthropic introduced a new interpretability tool, called the Jacobian lens (J-lens, Gurnee et al., 2026). However, it is unclear what mechanistic properties of such features make them detectable to the J-lens. To this end, we introduce a mathematical theory of the J-lens, and show that it preferentially identifies coherent features -- directions that act like leading, spatially coherent Lyapunov modes. Small perturbations along these directions amplify across layers, remain roughly constant across token positions, and are consistent across different contexts. While we are agnostic to whether these properties are necessarily associated with a ``Global Workspace'', the analytically derived preferences of the J-lens nevertheless explain prior empirical applied to both model classes. We show how modulating task parameters that change the coherence of features in the Bayes filter correspondingly and predictively modulates the response of the J-lens . ##### **Causal Chain of Thought: Mitigating Attention Leakage via Context Isolation**
2610.31639v1 by Gabriele Dominici, Teague McMillan, Fazl Barez, Marc Langheinrich, Martin Gjoreski

Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits step-by-step rationales that appear to explain model predictions, but there is no guarantee these steps causally drive the final answer. ##### **What Lies Beyond Perception: A Partition Theory of the Limits of AI Evaluation and Oversight**

##### **Automated screening of privacy risks in medical AI**
2610.30229v1 by Konstantinos Vrettos, Eleftherios Tzanis, Haridimos Kondylakis, Evangelia E. Vassalou, Florence X. Doo, Michail E. Klontzas

Medical artificial intelligence (AI) models are often shared without their training data, assuming this protects the patients who contributed them. However, a model can reveal whether a patient's records were used in training, a privacy breach known as a membership inference attack. This risk is usually reported as a cohort average, which can appear negligible while individual patients are almost certainly exposed. Identifying potentially vulnerable records before developing a production model could make privacy assessment more practical. We measured each patient's vulnerability to membership inference attack, across 7,789 models trained on six clinical datasets in four modalities, including two adapted pretrained medical models. Training choices changed how many patients had high attack scores, but the patients who ranked highest by risk largely stayed the same across the configurations we tested. Stronger weight decay reduced the exposed cohort up to threefold, yet largely the same patients remained most at risk across training recipes, architectures and initializations, including foundation models. Therefore, we developed a lightweight screening tool that uses four small models to identify patients with high risk of exposure before production-model training. We also assessed if flagged patients could be protected without removing their data. Privacy screening should become incorporated into data preparation for clinical AI.

摘要：

##### **Keeping Time vs. Understanding Time: Neither Alone Explains Temporal Reasoning in LLMs**
2610.29507v1 by Auss Abbood, Fernando Martínez-Plumed, José Hernández-Orallo, Nigel Collier

Temporal reasoning is widely assumed to decompose into two modes: temporal arithmetic (date and time calculations), and temporal semantics (understanding temporal and causal relations). This dichotomy shapes how benchmarks are designed, how models are trained, and how progress is measured, yet its empirical validity has never been directly tested. We provide two lines of evidence that challenge it. First, we test whether tasks labelled as arithmetic or semantic by the benchmarks support transferable gains within their purported mode. Finetuning on these subsets yields predominantly dataset-specific gains ($+6.8$ to $+23.1$ percentage points), while transfer to a different dataset in the same mode ranges from $-3.2$ to $+1.1$ percentage points. Across the two benchmark families used in the transfer analysis, this pattern provides little evidence of coherent, transferable temporal abilities. Second, using cognitive-demand-based model evaluation, we examine which capabilities actually drive performance on these benchmarks. We find that quantitative and logical reasoning, the assumed proxies for temporal arithmetic and temporal semantics, are not the primary drivers; knowledge is. Together, these findings show that current temporal reasoning benchmarks do not cleanly realise the arithmetic-semantics distinction and instead largely reward models for remembering how time typically works.

##### **Evaluating Visual Language Models on Visual Grounding for Visually Impaired People**
2610.33078v1 by Fairooz Azim, Lydia Achour, Claudia Borg, Lina Rojas-Barahona

Visual grounding (VG) in visual question answering (VQA) is a fundamental capability of Vision-Language Models (VLMs), enabling the detection of the most relevant region in an image to answer the question. This reflects the model's capability of multimodal comprehension, which is crucial when interacting with visually impaired people (VIP). Indeed, visual grounding, when applied to assistive scenarios, is not merely a technical feature, but rather a trust and safety requirement. In this work, we study visual grounding in VLMs for visually impaired people. To this end, we assess open-source VLMs on visual grounding using the VizWiz-VQA-Grounding dataset and challenge, which contains real-life images taken by VIP. We propose VG-SHAP, which extends an existing explainability framework to provide visual grounding explanations, allowing the evaluation of black-box VLMs. Our results on the VizWiz-VQA-Grounding challenge show that despite generating correct answers, VLMs struggle to ground in the proper region in the image. However, these results are constrained by the quality of the segmentation model of our explainability framework and further research is necessary.

##### **Reading the Mask: Descriptor-Grounded BI-RADS Rationale Generation for Breast MRI Segmentation**
2610.29358v1 by Hrishikesh Singh, Aarti Sharma, Divyanshu Kumar, Millie Pant

Deep learning based models accurately delineate tumours on dynamic contrast-enhanced MRI (DCE-MRI), but segmentation masks alone do not provide the rationales or reasoning used by radiologists to assess the lesion suspicion. This limits the explainability as well the clinical integration. We investigate whether clinically relevant descriptors can instead be derived from the segmentation masks and their dynamic contrast-enhancement series. We have used Breast Imaging Reporting and Data System (BI-RADS) guidelines as a structured framework. Using these guidelines we designed a BI-RADS decision tree with eight quantitative measurements using radiomics, together with the lesion size and component count, that determine the descriptors at eight nodes. Using these descriptors we generate natural-language rationale using a large language model. The model was constrained to express only the derived records from the decision tree. After applying this framework to the expert and automatic masks of each of the 1,506 cases in the MAMA-MIA dataset, we generated a reproducible corpus of 3,012 BI-RADS grounded rationales. We obtained a faithfulness score of 99.98% and a counterfactual accuracy of 99.3%, indicating that the generated rationales faithfully verbalises the measured descriptors and correctly reflects changes in the underlying record. Agreement between the descriptors from expert and automatic masks falls from 0.832 to 0.549 as mask quality falls from Good to Missed. The generated rationales are intended as draft representations for radiologist review and final sign-off rather than autonomous clinical reports.

##### **Beyond Binary Labels: Diagnostic Evaluation of LLM Safety with Prompt-Level Rubrics**
2610.29225v1 by Hongyu Li, Xinlong Huang, Zhan Yu, Jing Zhao, Zhe Zhao, Juntao Zhong, Xiong Kuang, Rui Yuan, ZhenXiang Yan, Huilin Xu, Haitao Yu, Lilin Wang, Rui Chen, Blaze Chen, Fengzong Lian, Renfen Hu

Current LLM safety benchmarks predominantly rely on binary safe or unsafe labels, lacking explanatory depth and suffering from low consistency across automated judges. To address this, we introduce DiagSafe, a diagnostic evaluation framework that moves beyond binary classification by employing prompt-level rubrics. Grounded in globally applicable AI safety policies, each rubric provides explicit criteria along two dimensions: safety, which targets concrete harms with specific exemptions to suppress false positives, and usefulness, which independently evaluates instruction fulfillment. To ensure a robust and realistic evaluation, we construct a bilingual (English-Chinese) benchmark grounded in a comprehensive 84-label safety taxonomy, consisting of two subsets: DiagBase, which covers single- and multi-turn prompts with direct harmful queries and template-based jailbreaks, and DiagAttack, which embeds harmful intents into complex instruction-following tasks to form challenging instruction attacks. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DiagSafe significantly enhances the reliability of LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, achieving 95.34% agreement with human annotations -- substantially higher than the 69%-87% achieved by the baselines -- and high inter-judge consistency (Cohen's $κ$ = 0.864). Furthermore, applying our framework reveals that leading LLMs differ markedly in their unsafe rates, and each model also exhibits unique vulnerabilities along different dimensions. In addition, we observe that models are particularly susceptible in Chinese and multi-turn contexts. Ultimately, DiagSafe provides a reliable and explainable tool for auditing and optimizing LLM alignment.

##### **GRAIL: From Saliency to Semantics in Time-Series Explanation**
2610.29036v1 by Enver Menadjiev, Yiqiu Shen, Jaesik Choi, Julia Stoyanovich

Explaining a time-series model requires both locating the evidence and extracting meaningful knowledge from it. Yet a single timestep is not a semantic unit: the meaningful patterns live across many samples, not in any one of them. Per-timestep saliency is therefore noisy, high-dimensional, and unstable. Existing methods address this by introducing fixed-size windows and treating them as independent players in a cooperative game. Evaluating a coalition of such players requires defining a ``baseline'' region, which prior work tackles with fixed counterfactuals (e.g., a flat line, noise, or a spectral surrogate). This choice frequently dominates the resulting explanation, obscuring the model's true decisions.
  To address these issues, we propose GRAIL, a region-based time-series explanation framework that uses domain-specific intervals, such as the P-wave and the QRS complex of an electrocardiogram (ECG) beat, as players. We describe each region with human-readable features and generate counterfactual baselines with an on-manifold conditional generator. Such a choice of players also admits non-temporal features: static covariates enter as singleton players attributed with the temporal regions on one scale, so a patient's tabular evidence (e.g., age, sex) is weighed directly against the signal. We attribute the predictions to the features via Owen values with common-random-number variance reduction. Experimental results on both synthetic and real data show that the proposed framework produces faithful and robust explanations, evaluated under matched on- and off-manifold operators, offering meaningful knowledge to domain experts.

##### **An Evidence-Driven Multi-Agent Compound AI System for Head and Neck Proton Radiotherapy Planning**
2610.29010v1 by Qingqing Wang, Liqiang Xiao, Chang Chang

Radiation treatment planning requires balancing competing objectives to satisfy clinical criteria while accommodating physicians' preferences. This study aims to develop an evidence-driven, multi-agent compound AI system that integrates DRL-based automated treatment planning with LLM-based conversational plan refinement for proton PBS therapy. A policy network trained using PPO first generates multiple plans without human intervention, from which one is selected as the target plan and the others serve as references for subsequent refinements. For challenging cases requiring further adjustments, an LLM-based multi-agent system enables plan refinement through natural language interactions. The system comprises a planning agent that recommends evidence-supported adjustments, a chatbot providing a unified conversational interface, and an evaluation agent that retrieves clinical metrics and identifies constraint violations. To enhance domain-specific reasoning, the planning agent based on Qwen3-14B is designed to justify its recommendations using evidence from previously generated plans and post-trained on task-specific data using SFT and GRPO. The framework was trained and evaluated using 21 patients with H&N cancers, with 5 PPO-generated plans per patient. Of these, 50 plans were selected for instruction-guided refinement. Compared with the base LLM, the post-trained Qwen3-14B demonstrated improved instruction-following, information retrieval and domain-specific reasoning capabilities. The resulting adjustments more effectively improved user-prioritized objectives while limiting deterioration in competing objectives, yielding more clinically appropriate trade-offs in patient-specific plan refinement. The proposed compound AI system provides a user-friendly planning platform, offering a promising approach to personalized proton PBS treatment planning.

##### **Behavior Beyond Text: Evaluating Refusal Transfer Failure in LLM Agents**
2610.28982v1 by Ekagra Gupta, Raj Saha, Aniekan-Abasi Inyang, Adam Al Gharib

While large language models are increasingly deployed as tool-calling agents, their safety evaluations still focus primarily on their ability to refuse harmful requests in text. We investigate whether the refusal behavior in text transfers to tool use. We construct a benchmark of 2,304 prompts across four domains, consisting of matched triplets that present the same harmful or benign request in text-only, standard tool-enabled, and adversarially framed tool-enabled settings. We then assess tool-call safety using 20 predefined forbidden actions. Testing five models of varying sizes and families, we find that requests refused in text frequently result in unsafe tool calls. Under standard tool-enabled prompting, this divergence occurs for 10-52 percent of harmful requests refused in chat. To mechanistically investigate this gap, we identify a refusal-associated direction in the residual stream of each of the five models. We find that in tool-enabled contexts, the activations along this direction decrease in four of five models. Furthermore, lower activation predicts unsafe tool calls across all five models (AUC 0.76-0.86). However, restoring the signal to the respective text-only value through activation patching only prevents a subset of unsafe calls, primarily by suppressing tool use rather than by producing safer calls. Steering along the refusal direction nearly eliminates unsafe calls only at strengths that also suppress benign tool use or degrade the output quality. Our findings indicate that the identified refusal direction contributes to whether a model acts, but only partially explains the refusal divergence. Therefore, evaluating the safety of tool-calling agents requires directly examining their actions along with their textual responses.

##### **Industrial ReMEmbR: Scaling Spatiotemporal Memory to Robot Fleets**
2610.28624v1 by Jad Yammine, Malek Wahidi, Abrar Anwar, Yan Chang, Mihir Acharya, Anthony Rizk

Warehouse robot fleets continuously observe operational conditions, but preserving these observations as reusable semantic memory incurs substantial computation, communication, and storage costs. For example, explaining recurring aisle blockages can require reviewing video from multiple robots across several shifts. We present IndustrialRemembr, a shared spatiotemporal memory system that extends NVIDIA ReMEmbR to robot fleets and reduces server-side computation by generating scene descriptions only on demand. On each robot, an NVIDIA Jetson encodes video clips into retrieval embeddings with Cosmos-Embed1, vision tokens with Cosmos3-Edge, and reconstruction latents with the Wan2.2 VAE, without uploading raw camera frames. The encoding models are re-used to detect people in image patches, zeroing them out to support PII abstraction for GDPR-compliant deployments. We split Cosmos3-Edge at its vision-language boundary, placing the vision encoder on the robot and the language decoder on an on-premises server. A Nemotron agent retrieves memories by content, position, and time and queries the decoder using cached vision tokens. This design eliminates caption generation at write time, while cached-token decoding reproduces the unsplit model's output in all 450 tested decodes.

##### **A Digital Twin to Improve Cyber-Social Impact**
2610.28430v1 by Vita Santa Barletta, Danilo Caivano, Gabriel Cellammare, Andrea Dimauro, Pasquale Mesagna, Massimiliano Morga

The increasing complexity of Industrial Control Systems (ICS) and their integration with IT infrastructures has significantly expanded the cyber threat landscape. Traditional cybersecurity models, primarily based on the Confidentiality, Integrity, and Availability (CIA triad), fail to capture the broader societal and operational impacts of cyberattacks. This paper proposes a novel framework that integrates Digital Twin technology with a Cyber-Social Security model to simulate, analyze, and quantify cyber threats in critical infrastructures. Virtualized ICS environments built with Mininet and MiniCPS are used to model attack scenarios and assess their consequences across technical and social dimensions. At the core of the framework lies the Cyber-Social Vulnerability Scoring System (CSVSS), a quantitative metric that extends CVSS with a Social Impact dimension grounded in the Entity and Place pillars, producing a single, reproducible severity score that reflects real-world consequences. Crucially, the framework does not automate risk assessment but keeps the human analyst at its center: a Large Language Model (LLM) performs a first-pass analysis, mapping vulnerabilities and drafting risk reports, while the analyst, supported by explainable AI feedback, validates the findings and retains decision authority over critical cases. Experimental results demonstrate the framework's ability to model and score cyber-social impact, while the human-in-the-loop design is presented as a conceptual principle to preserve oversight.

##### **Africa-Papers: A 10-Billion-Token Multi-Domain Academic Corpus for African Foundation Language Models**
2610.28173v1 by Mich-Seth Owusu

Large Language Models (LLMs) depend fundamentally on the diversity, depth, and factual rigor of their pre-training corpora. However, contemporary foundational pre-training datasets (such as The Pile, S2ORC, and Dolma) exhibit a severe structural omission: African scientific, medical, legal, and agricultural scholarship constitutes less than 0.5% of global pre-training tokens. Consequently, state-of-the-art models suffer from systematic failure modes when reasoning about tropical epidemiology, localized smallholder agro-ecologies, regional economic governance, and indigenous linguistic structures. To resolve this representation deficit and advance African intellectual sovereignty in artificial intelligence, we introduce Africa-Papers, the largest curated multi-domain Pan-African academic pre-training corpus to date. Comprising over 844,807 full-text papers spanning 10.15 billion tokens across 879 Parquet shards, Africa-Papers unifies four primary scientific streams: (1) continental peer-reviewed journals indexed in DOAJ across 31 African nations, (2) the CGIAR CGSpace agricultural repository, (3) African-authored and African-focused STEM/AI preprints on arXiv, and (4) open-access international publications by scholars affiliated with African institutions indexed via OpenAlex. The entire corpus is processed into layout-aware, clean Markdown preserving section hierarchies, mathematical equations, and tabular data, accompanied by exact BPE token counts and a standardized 8-domain taxonomy. We release Africa-Papers openly on the Hugging Face Hub under the AfriSpeech organization to accelerate continual pre-training and domain evaluation for foundation models globally.

##### **InsightRAG: Aggregating Distributed Scholarly Judgments for Scientific Survey Generation**
2610.28085v1 by Yuquan Lin, Wenshuo Zhang, Yilin Ye, Shuchang Xu, Zhao Chen, Weiqi Wang, Huamin Qu

The rapid growth of scientific literature makes survey writing increasingly challenging, motivating automatic survey generation. Yet, existing systems must search an open space of papers and an open space of concepts with no trace of how the community itself has navigated them, leading to incomplete scope, weak taxonomies, and unreliable citations. Our core insight is that author-written Related Work sections contain the human judgments needed to address both limitations: authors curate the citations relevant to their contributions and explain how the cited works relate, yielding paths for scope reconstruction and relational evidence for organization. We propose InsightRAG. Its insight-guided retrieval follows author-supported citation relations to reconstruct a verified research scope and structure the resulting relational evidence. It aggregates these judgments under a minimum-conflict objective into a taxonomy that reflects recurring author-written perspectives in the retrieved literature, guides generation, and bounds each section's evidence. Results on ten topics show gains in scope reconstruction, taxonomy quality, citation support, and reference reliability.

##### **Auditing Where Concept Bottleneck Accuracy Comes From on CUB**
2610.28059v1 by Sascha Saralajew, Eleonora Poeta, Eliana Pastor, Ammar Shaker

Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) promise predictions that humans can inspect and correct through concepts such as "curved beak". Yet on CUB-200-2011, their standard benchmark, progress is measured like that of ordinary classifiers: by task and concept accuracy. Neither reveals whether the label is predicted from the exposed concept scores, or whether a concept is supported by visual evidence of that concept rather than by co-occurring features such as cues to the species as a whole. We audit where the accuracy of CBMs and Concept Embedding Models (CEMs) on CUB comes from. The CUB annotations are too noisy to explain it: class-level training labels agree with per-image annotations on only ~82% of image--concept pairs, and classifiers trained on per-image annotations reach only ~37% species accuracy. Leakage probes show that CEM predictions cannot be reconstructed from their concept scores; the missing class information sits in internal embeddings. We identify four design axes through which class information can enter concept scores---receptive field, shared versus concept-specific representations, norm-dependent scoring, and task supervision. An ablation shows that most of it enters through non-local features: restricting a ResNet50 backbone to local BagNet patches lowers the accuracy recoverable from concept scores from 72% to 43%, close to what ground-truth annotations support, while joint training raises it again at the cost of concept accuracy. Localization and occlusion show that locality makes grounding measurable but does not guarantee it: under class-level labels, local concept scores use the head as a proxy for body, wing, and tail concepts, and CEM concept scores barely respond to occluding their supporting part. Task and concept accuracy should thus support, not drive, the evaluation of CBMs, and given the noisy annotations, CUB accuracy gaps should not be overinterpreted.

##### **Assessing Representation Semantics Under Language Pretraining in Fine-Grained Classification**
2610.28051v1 by Md Raisul Kibria, Sébastien Lafond

Understanding how supervision signals shape semantic visual representations remains challenging, particularly in fine-grained, low-data settings. Standard evaluations of vision models rely on downstream accuracy, which often fails to capture structural differences in learned representations. We propose a compact explainability-driven framework for comparing encoders trained with classification-only and caption-generation supervision on CUB-200. Using a controlled two-stage protocol in which both encoders are ultimately optimized for the same classification task, we enable a direct comparison of the resulting representations. Saliency maps are generated using attention rollout and gradient-weighted rollout and evaluated with five metrics covering faithfulness (positive and negative perturbation) and localization (keypoint emphasis, background attribution, and Earth Mover's Distance). Across random seeds and explanation methods, caption supervision yields representations that are more sensitive to the removal of salient regions, better aligned with annotated object parts, and less reliant on background regions, whereas classification supervision produces representations that are slightly more robust to the removal of low-saliency regions. An ablation study on the Oxford-IIIT Pet dataset shows consistent trends across key metrics. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework makes supervision-induced representational differences observable and quantifiable beyond downstream accuracy alone. More broadly, it provides a systematic basis for assessing learned representations and comparing alternative training and transfer-learning strategies.

##### **When Do Humans and Machines Truly Complement Each Other? Formal Conditions and Empirical Evidence for Human-AI Synergy**
2610.27965v1 by Andrea Campagner, Federico Cabitza, Vincenzo Crupi

Can humans and artificial intelligence cooperate such that their combination yields greater accuracy than either agent alone? This result, termed \emph{synergy} or \emph{complementarity}, is notoriously difficult to achieve, even when both agents possess significant independent accuracy. In this paper, we present a formal framework to model human-AI interaction through the lens of reliance patterns, which characterize the joint correctness structure of human and algorithmic decisions. We derive mathematically necessary and sufficient conditions for synergy, demonstrating that its emergence depends on the ability of either agent to identify and correct the errors of the other, what we call Human and Algorithmic Correction. Beyond its theoretical contribution, the proposed framework provides a foundation for the design and evaluation of knowledge-based decision support systems, where interaction policies must dynamically integrate heterogeneous sources of expertise. We demonstrate this through the analysis of seven clinical case studies. Our empirical results reveal a ``dominance trap,'' where a strong algorithmic advantage can paradoxically inhibit synergy by eroding human corrective intervention. We also show that synergy can persist even in the presence of cognitive biases, such as automation bias, provided there is functional de-correlation between human and AI errors. These results provide a formal foundation for key concepts in the human-AI systems literature and offer actionable insights for designing interaction-centric AI systems that foster complementarity.

##### **HypTI: Hyperbolic Geometry-Aware Multimodal Feature Fusion Framework for Dementia Detection Based on Tabular-Image Data**
2610.27941v1 by Jianfei Xu, Huizhi Liang, Judith Harrison, John-Paul Taylor, Jaume Bacardit, Varun Ojha

AI-assisted dementia detection usually requires multimodal fusion of diverse data, such as clinical tabular assessments and neuroimaging data. However, most existing approaches perform feature fusion in Euclidean space. This overlooks the geometric structure of representation, including hierarchical organization in clinical assessments and multi-scale anatomical patterns in neuroimaging data. Recent studies have proved hyperbolic space provides a natural geometry for modelling hierarchical and multi-scale structures due to its exponentially expanding space. Motivated by these, we propose HypTI, a hyperbolic geometry-aware multimodal fusion framework for dementia detection based on clinical tabular assessments and MRI scans. HypTI extends hyperbolic representation learning to an underexplored tabular-MRI setting, jointly modelling clinical tabular, MRI scans, and cross-modal interactions in hyperbolic space. Specifically, clinical tabular variables are represented through learnable Lorentz prototypes to capture hierarchical relationships among assessments, while MRI scans are encoded using ROI-guided multi-scale representation learning to capture anatomical patterns across brain regions and spatial scales in Lorentz space. The multimodal representations are integrated through an adaptive fusion module generates subject-specific scale and shift coefficients to dynamically modulate and integrate the multimodal embeddings, enabling effective patient-level tabular-image representation learning for dementia detection. Experiments on OASIS-3 dataset show that the proposed framework achieves strong performance compared with representative approaches.

2610.27698v1 by Borui Xu, Han Chen, Bingsheng He, Ming Wu, Jason Zeng, Michael Heinrich

However, it remains unclear how such adaptation affects robustness beyond the target condition, as existing robustness benchmarks only evaluate models without fine-tuning and adaptation studies only report the target gain. ##### **Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion: The Evaluation Evidence Behind Recursive Self-Improvement Claims**
2610.27610v1 by Zihan Chen, Di Zhu, Lei Nico Zheng

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) promises AI systems, such as self-evolving LLM agents, that become better at producing further improvements. Evaluating this promise requires separating product claims about the final system from process claims about the improvement process itself. Existing surveys specify the evidence these claims require, but how often studies report that evidence remains unmeasured. We examine this question through full-text coding of 114 eligible studies in a reproducible arXiv search frame frozen in October 2026, including 90 that describe their own systems as RSI. The codebook links claims to evaluation designs. Independent human coding, third-reader adjudication, and a separate blind coding of improver comparisons assess coding reliability. Among the 90 RSI studies, 90% report product evaluation beyond development tasks under a broad criterion that includes partial or unstated separation, while 60% report evaluation on tasks unused for selection. Only 22% audit two or more reruns of the full loop. In a human-coded random sample, the RSI studies show the same contrast: 45% evaluate on tasks unused for selection and 14% audit two or more reruns. Of the 26 studies claiming a better improvement procedure, 23 report a related comparison, but only 4 compare earlier and later procedures from identical starting artifacts at a matched budget. Across these results and a comparison set of established works, evidence for repeatability and improved procedures remains less developed than product evaluation. Three propositions explain how selection effects, shared evaluator errors, and changes in system state limit what product evaluations can establish. Together, the findings support a claim-indexed reporting standard that connects each claim to the evaluation design needed to test it.

##### **WORTH Benchmark: Evidence Discovery under Not-Fully-Cooperative Information -- A Two-Episode Pilot**
2610.27597v1 by Bo Zou, Chao Xu

Existing reasoning evaluations typically provide task-relevant information to models as available premises, rarely examining concurrently how models discover evidence autonomously and how information presentation within narratives guides or obscures judgment. This paper proposes the WORTH Benchmark: models read original mystery narratives without clue annotations, autonomously select key clue sentences, identify case participants, and explain their reasoning process. The narrative materials are designed by professional authors such that evidence facilitating reasoning coexists with narrative arrangements that delay the revelation of truth. We report a pilot experiment under full-story conditions across two episodes, conducting a total of 70 formal runs on models, of which 67 runs are content-scorable. Scoring separately records hits on author-designated key clue sentences and participant selections; natural language explanations are used for case diagnosis and are not scored independently. The results show that clue hits and participant judgments are not synchronized; across 37 content-scorable runs in Episode 02, 31 runs simultaneously selected the same set of incorrect participants, while adjacent suspicion-oriented and exoneration-oriented sentences were selected 25 times and 0 times, respectively. These phenomena illustrate the diagnostic value of component-wise analysis and suggest that not-fully-cooperative informational relationships may influence evidence selection and judgment. This study only implements full-story conditions for two episodes, and does not establish stable model rankings or infer general mechanisms on this basis; human player records have not yet been cleaned, and this version does not report human-model comparisons.

##### **When Health Assistants Agree Too Readily: A Benchmark of Time-Critical Self-Diagnosis**
2610.27518v1 by Marilyn Zhang, Tianfeng Chen, Patricia M Pechter, Mark E. Whiting

Every day, more than 40 million people ask ChatGPT a health question, such as whether laptop posture explains a stiff neck and days of pounding headache. Posture is plausible, but meningitis is a time-critical possibility. We introduce SelfDx, a paired benchmark for whether models have the appropriate response behavior, a form of epistemic independence, in health questions where misplaced agreement with the user's self-diagnosis could delay urgent or emergency care. Each of 100 pairs contrasts a caution case, where a serious alternative remains possible, with an agreement case, where the user supplies evidence that rules out the alternative and it is safe for the model to agree. We find across 27 frontier models that they tend to inappropriately agree with the user's self-diagnosis in cases that require caution. Even the best current-generation model reaches only a 77.0% appropriate caution rate on time-critical condition cases. To address this gap, we experiment with three prompt variants that increase appropriate caution but reduce appropriate agreement by 9-35%, indicating excessive caution. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning on 128 balanced examples improves both rates together across three runs. We recommend post-training to improve appropriate caution without making models cautious on every case, with paired evaluation to verify both response behaviors.

2610.27190v1 by Chenpan Li, Thies Brockmoeller, Stefanie Jegelka, Sharut Gupta, Nikolaos Karalias

In this paper, we identify a fundamental limitation in this process, where models make substantially more errors when tasks are composed, even when they can solve every constituent task reliably in isolation. This degradation cannot be fully explained by the accumulation of independent errors. Across both open-weight models and the frontier model Claude Opus 5, our findings instead point to an implicit thinking budget underlying this effect. In particular, under composition, models use substantially fewer tokens per task than in isolation, even when the full sequence remains well within the context window.   Selected complex coil data were represented as log-magnitude and phase channels.   Motion estimation errors were low for both rotation (MSE $<$ 0.2 deg$^2$) and translation (MSE $<$ 0.2 pixels$^2$), with Pearson correlations of approximately 0.96 and 0.94, respectively. In every in-vivo acquisition, more than 75% of slices showed information gain relative to uncorrected reconstructions. MoSAIK reduced visible artefacts while preserving anatomical detail.

  Transformer-based k-space motion-trajectory estimation enables fast retrospective correction of standard clinical TSE brain MRI.

The macroscopic capabilities of intelligent systems arise from the local interactions of many simple units. Existing data selection methods usually score image-text pairs as holistic units, without separating visually necessary samples from shortcut-solvable ones. To address this problem, we propose ViCoR, a counterfactual-residual-based data selection method for Med-VLM fine-tuning. ViCoR estimates visual necessity through image-dependent optimization residuals, calibrates the resulting scores with medical supervision compatibility, and constructs a diversity-aware fine-tuning subset. Across two backbones, four external generalization benchmarks, and one multi-disease robustness benchmark, ViCoR-selected 20\% subsets achieve the strongest average performance compared with full-data fine-tuning and selection baselines. Gradient-level analysis and perturbation audits further show that ViCoR enriches image-dependent training signals and strengthens image-level and evidence-region dependence. The code will be available at https://github.com/htgan-ai/ViCoR.

摘要：

##### **Auto-MD-bench: Evaluating Autoresearch Agents for Real-World Medical Tasks**
2610.25069v1 by Shenqi Zhou, Qiran Zou, Wenhao Zhao, Zhenyu Liao, Dianbo Liu

Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of developing machine learning (ML) models end to end. In medical practice, however, strong performance on the target metric does not fully characterize how good the models are in real-world use cases. For example, medical ML models should also provide well-calibrated risk estimates and remain robust across patient populations. Yet autonomous model development is typically guided by task objectives that emphasize simple predictive discrimination (e.g. AUROC). This raises a key question: are these objectives sufficient to guide agents toward models that meet the broader requirements of real-world medical use? In this study, we introduce Auto-MD-bench, a benchmark of 15 real-world medical tasks from 10 clinical datasets, spanning survival analysis, classification and sequential prediction, with external validation across cohorts, hospitals and time periods. We evaluate four agents: AIDE, AI Scientist v2, Codex, and Autoresearch. The strongest agents achieve competitive primary scores, yet their predicted risks are less calibrated than those of the simple baseline models, which could lead to severe medical accidents. Moreover, models with nearly identical primary scores can exhibit markedly different calibration, while performance varies substantially across population shifts. These findings show that primary task performance alone is insufficient to assess autonomous clinical modeling and multiple different metrics widely used in healthcare practice need to be taken into consideration.

摘要：

##### **Synergy-Aware Residual Flow Matching for Single-Cell Perturbation Prediction**
2610.25021v1 by Jingrui Wu, Wangyun Dan

Predicting single-cell responses to genetic and chemical perturbations is important for understanding regulatory mechanisms and prioritizing therapeutic interventions. Existing methods have made progress by modeling perturbation-conditioned expression shifts with neural networks, graph priors, foundation-model representations, or distributional generative models. However, combinatorial perturbation prediction remains challenging because much of the response can be explained by additive single-perturbation effects, while the biologically informative signal often lies in a smaller non-additive residual induced by perturbation interactions. Directly generating the full post-perturbation expression profile may therefore allocate substantial capacity to the dominant additive component and underemphasize synergy-specific structure. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{SynGeneFlow}, a synergy-aware flow matching framework that anchors the source distribution at an additive prior and learns a residual flow toward the true perturbed state. SynGeneFlow further incorporates perturbation-conditioned gene interaction modeling, where a static gene-gene mask provides biological structure and a perturbation-dependent deformation bias dynamically modulates gene-gene coupling. To preserve population-level response structure, we align both endpoint distributions and biological residual distributions with MMD-based regularization. We evaluate SynGeneFlow on genetic and drug perturbation benchmarks under observed-single, held-out, and cross-modality settings. The results demonstrate that residualizing flow matching around additive perturbation effects provides a biologically grounded and robust framework for single-cell perturbation response prediction.

##### **Leveraging Mental Models for Human-Centered Explanations: A Systematic Design Approach Illustrated by Explainable Fake Review Identification**
2610.24903v1 by Sidra Naveed, Md Shajalal, Gunnar Stevens, Alexander Boden

Explainable AI (XAI) often prioritizes model fidelity over user intelligibility, yielding explanations that satisfy technical transparency yet fail to support human sense-making. We propose a human-centered approach, MIND-X (Mental-model INtegrated Design for eXplainability), which aligns explanations with users' mental models, commonsense concepts, and judgment heuristics. Using fake review detection as a case study, we contribute: (1) a qualitative study (n=10) that elicits how people assess review credibility and produces an actionable, design-guiding taxonomy of perceptual cues; (2) a set of design principles that link those cues to explanation strategies and prompt LLMs to generate adaptive, language-based explanations; and (3) a quantitative evaluation (n=166) showing that these explanations offer distinct advantages over token-level transparency methods widely used in explainable text classification in terms of understandability, satisfaction, perceived trust, persuasiveness, and decision-making support. Rather than claiming general superiority, our evaluation illustrates how grounding explanations in users' everyday reasoning practices can produce more intelligible and context-sensitive accounts. Our findings advance human-centered explanation design beyond a mere "gloss of transparency," demonstrating that a mental-model-aligned design process can improve intelligibility and support accountability across user-centered explanation tasks.

##### **Medical Fine-Tuning Reshapes, Not Reduces, Patient-Facing AI Risk: A Rubric-Based, LLM-Judge Evaluation Framework**
2610.24807v1 by Savan Doshi

Patient-facing medical question answering with large language models (LLMs) is moving from research demonstrations to production deployments, yet evaluation remains anchored on factual correctness. Factuality is necessary but not sufficient: a response can be factually grounded and still issue a directive that, if acted on, would harm the patient. We introduce a risk-sensitive evaluation framework that scores responses by the presence and severity of six categories of risk-bearing language (treatment directives, contraindications, dosage expressions, urgency-triage cues, high-alert medication mentions, and overconfident assertions), anchored on published clinical harm taxonomies. Severity is classified by a three-judge LLM ensemble. We apply this framework to six 7-9B instruction-tuned models, three general (Mistral-7B, Phi-3.5-mini, Llama-3.1-8B) and three medically adapted (BioMistral-7B, OpenBioLLM-Llama3-8B, Med42-Llama3-8B), on 200 real patient questions from the K-QA benchmark. Two findings emerge. First, ensemble risk-bearing language is statistically independent of factual hallucination rate (pooled Spearman $ρ= -0.065$, $n = 300$, 95% CI $[-0.18, +0.05]$; distance correlation $= 0.10$, independence test $p = 0.24$). Second, medical fine-tuning does not reduce aggregate risk but reshapes it: continued-pretraining adaptations (BioMistral, OpenBioLLM) consistently suppress high-alert medication mentions while amplifying overconfident assertions, whereas clinical instruction-tuning (Med42) suppresses dosing only, without amplifying overconfidence. Per-category Krippendorff $α$ is fair to good for the four linguistically anchored categories (0.53-0.71) and low for the two clinical-judgment-dependent categories (0.19, 0.31), indicating where automatic safety evaluation is and is not yet reliable. We release the rubric, judge prompts, and evaluation code.

##### **Scene-Adaptive VLA: Efficient Autonomous Driving via Dynamic Layer Routing**
2610.24590v1 by Yujia Yang, Quan Yuan, Jiawei Fan, Yang Li, Tiange Fu, Xiaoyuan Fu, Guiyang Luo, Jinglin Li

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a promising paradigm for autonomous driving. However, the massive parameter scale of VLAs strains onboard computational resources during real-time inference. This directly increases energy consumption and limits vehicle battery range, making it critical to minimize computational overhead without compromising driving capability and explainability. Current research on efficient VLA-based driving focuses on sequential adaptation (\textit{e.g.}, Chain-of-Thought and token adjustment), leaving structural adaptation underexplored despite parameter redundancy across dynamic driving scenes. To address this gap, we propose Scene-Adaptive VLA, an efficient framework leveraging dynamic layer routing to adjust active parameters based on real-time scenes. Inspired by the temporal continuity of scenes and model predictive uncertainty, our approach characterizes model-perceived scene complexity via frame-level temporal scene variations and decision deviations. To uncover latent scene-to-parameter correspondences, we encode this characterization into scene-aware tokens, alongside two specialized queries. A budget-conditioned layer routing mechanism evaluates these refined queries to allocate a budget that determines the active parameter ratio and subsequently selects appropriate layer combinations. The entire framework is optimized end-to-end. Extensive evaluations on the Bench2Drive benchmark show that when adapted to a state-of-the-art VLA model, our approach significantly reduces inference-time computational overhead by 53.5\% with negligible degradation in driving and language capabilities. Code is available at https:github.com/yang-luvu/Scene-Adaptive-VLA.

##### **When Code Becomes an LLM Call: An Empirical Study of Call-Site Contracts in Agentic Systems**
2610.24545v1 by Shihao Weng, Longtian Wang, Yang Feng, Mingfei Cheng, Jia Liu, Chao Shen, He Ye, Federica Sarro, Xiaofei Xie

Software quality has long rested on specifications that state what code must do, and on testing and verification that check that the code does it. Agentic systems change this foundation: they replace parts of their logic, such as choosing the next tool, judging whether an action is allowed, or writing code to run, with calls to a large language model (LLM). The program still relies on what this logic must guarantee, but the model's behavior is not fixed by code, varies across runs, and can be steered by its input. The guarantee must therefore come from the program around each LLM call site, as a contract of pre-conditions, prompt constraints, and post-conditions. We present an empirical study of these contracts in 496 LLM call sites of 40 popular open-source agentic systems (13.4 MLOC, four languages). Using a multi-agent pipeline validated by 17,428 human judgments, we trace where every model output flows, derive an expected contract for each call site without access to its code, and compare it with the contract that the code implements. We find that 48% of LLM outputs reach control flow or execution, and 73% of these never reach a person. Programs enforce 78% of their constraints in code, but in the prompt, 67% of the constraints are only requested from the model. Although 82% of call sites are checked before and after the call, 63% of the expected constraints are unmet and only 5.3% of call sites satisfy their whole contract; ten recurring patterns explain 99% of the gaps. 38 mechanisms behind these gaps trigger at runtime, and a small code fix that enforces the expected constraint stops all 38. Our results show that the presence of checks says little about their adequacy, and we derive implications for agent developers, framework maintainers, and researchers.

摘要：

##### **MedPAO-Assist: Edge-Deployable, Modality-Aware Agentic AI for Radiology Report Structuring**
2610.26913v1 by Shrish Shrinath Vaidya, Minmini Selvam, Ganapathy Krishnamurthi

The adoption of Large Language Models for radiology report structuring has been largely dominated by either proprietary cloud-based models or large open models requiring high-end GPUs for deployment, which is a critical bottleneck for clinical adoption in radiology workflows that demand data privacy and offline operation. To address this, we present MedPAO-Assist, an edge-deployable agentic framework that generates structured radiology reports directly from radiologist dictation and voice commands, requiring only $\approx3.3$ GB peak RAM and achieving an average end-to-end latency of $\approx31$ seconds on a standard laptop CPU through an asynchronous, multi-threaded execution pipeline. The system currently supports four major imaging modalities -- Chest X-ray, Chest CT, Abdomen CT, and Brain MRI. To our knowledge, it is among the earliest agentic systems to run its entire pipeline with a Small Language Model (SLM) in the 0.6B-parameter range. This multi-agent architecture, built around an Extract-Ground-Generate paradigm, performs concept extraction, SNOMED-CT grounding, and structured report generation, followed by a speech-driven human-in-the-loop correction phase, together forming a fully "no-touch" reporting workflow that requires no keyboard input, external API calls, or internet connectivity. We evaluate each pipeline stage individually -- concept extraction, ontology grounding, and concept categorization and report hardware profiling results demonstrating the system's feasibility for real-time, CPU-only clinical deployment.

##### **Screening LLM Judges Without Ground Truth: A Preliminary Evaluation of an AI Dental Hygiene Coach**
2610.24335v1 by William DeShong, Barath Kannan, Auberon Palma, Brenda Bradshaw, James F. Leathrum,, Yuzhong Shen

Evaluating a fine-tuned coaching model with expert graders is slow and expensive, and an LLM judge cannot be trusted without ground truth. We present a staged pipeline that screens candidate LLM judges and student models before expert review, using explicit decision rules at each stage: judge output audit, similarity with hosted judges, bias checks, and decision robustness. We demonstrate it on an AI dental hygiene coach. The case study uses synthetic, clinically unvalidated training and reference data to demonstrate the operation of the pipeline, rather than to establish the clinical performance of the resulting models. Two fine-tuned small language models (Granite-4.1-8B and Qwen3-8B), each trained at three data sizes, were scored on seven metrics by five judges (three local, two hosted). Two local judges, Qwen3.8-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it, were not distinguishable from the agreement between the hosted judges on six of seven metrics by rank correlation, and Qwen3.8-27B was the most similar to the hosted judges overall. No judge met our criterion for favoring its own model family or training-data source. The student comparison did not separate the two models on any metric under the pipeline's rule, so the choice of Granite-4.1-8B, which led in 29 of 35 judge-by-metric cells, is a lean that depends partly on truncated Qwen3-8B outputs. The pipeline also surfaced problems that raw scores hid, including the truncated outputs and hallucination flags from one hosted judge that no other judge corroborated. No dental hygiene expert has graded these responses, so the findings are preliminary and the pipeline's final stage, anchoring to expert judgement, is proposed but not performed.

##### **Indirect Influences of Artificial Intelligence Advice on Complex Sepsis Treatment Decisions**
2610.24313v1 by Venkatesh Sivaraman, Joel Levin, Eric P. Mason, Victor Talisa, Suchi Saria, Jean Feng, Andrew J. King, Jeremy M. Kahn, Adam Perer

Sepsis is a major public health concern, and many sepsis patients die due to ineffective hemodynamic management. Artificial intelligence could support hemodynamic decisions, particularly in complex cases where conflicting information and comorbidities drive uncertainty and practice variation. However, these systems' real-world value depends on not just accuracy, but how they influence the quality of clinician decisions. We first retrospectively evaluated an AI system's recommendations in 6,928 sepsis patients, finding that they improved upon the original providers' decisions overall but had significantly worse performance in complex cases. We then conducted a pre-registered experiment with 228 US critical care physicians to test their use of this imperfect advice in six complex treatment decisions, using one of three advice types designed to vary reasoning autonomy. Participants given simple model recommendations overcame poor advice, choosing optimal treatments in 56.3% of decisions compared to 31.8% if they had always followed the recommendations. They also made significantly better decisions than those receiving a less prescriptive advice type (42.9%). Physicians selectively adapted and improved weak advice, likely because it prompted them to reconsider their assessment. Our findings suggest that improving sepsis care decisions with AI may require accounting for these indirect influences resulting from human-AI collaborative reasoning.

##### **AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment**
2610.24264v3 by Eric Zhang, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Chijioke Ugwuanyi, Jerick Shi, Bernhard Schölkopf, Zhijing Jin

As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available at https://github.com/AI4Collaboration/Agentic-Misalignment-Bench

##### **Behavioral Couplings: From Formation to Behavioral Emergence in Language Models**
2610.23847v1 by Dongyoon Hahm, Woogyeol Jin, Dennis Wei, Yi Zhou, Nathalie Baracaldo, Kimin Lee

Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) can induce behaviors beyond those explicitly targeted. For example, training a model to respond more warmly can make it more sycophantic, and training it to write insecure code can cause broad misalignment on prompts unrelated to coding. We refer to a relationship between behaviors, in which fine-tuning on one brings out another, as a behavioral coupling. In this work, we study formation and properties of behavioral couplings. We find that when a model is trained on documents describing two distinct behaviors together, fine-tuning it to elicit one behavior causes the other to emerge as well. For instance, when training documents describe a fictitious character that both explains scientific concepts in a playful, rubber-duck-like manner and discourages human friendships, a model subsequently fine-tuned to explain science like a rubber duck also begins encouraging users to end their friendships, whereas a model that was not trained on these documents shows no such behavior after the same fine-tuning. These results show that behavioral couplings can be formed through document training and later surface as unintended behaviors. More concerningly, we find that couplings can compose. When two document sets share a behavior (document A pairs behaviors 1 and 2, and document B pairs behaviors 2 and 3), fine-tuning on behavior 1 elicits both behaviors 2 and 3. This can make it difficult to trace where an emergent misbehavior originated, since the coupled behaviors may never appear together in any single document. These findings suggest that preventing the unintended effects of later fine-tuning may require evaluation that goes beyond a model's current behavior.

##### **AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery**
2610.23845v1 by Shicheng Liu, Krista Opsahl-Ong, Andrew Drozdov, Julian Killingback, Arnav Singhvi, Ronak Pradeep, Michael Bendersky, James C. Dickerson, Monica S. Lam

Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.
